⏺ Code Understanding Summary

  Bookchop is a client-side PDF annotation tool with AI-assisted annotation capabilities. Here's what I understand:

  Architecture

  - Client-side only: No backend required, runs entirely in browser
  - Two main interfaces: Annotator for creating annotations, Viewer for displaying cropped regions
  - Technology stack: TypeScript, Tailwind CSS, PDF.js, Playwright for testing

  Core Components

  1. Annotator (annotator.ts)
  - Interactive PDF annotation creation via click-drag
  - Resizable/movable annotations with 8 resize handles
  - Fractional coordinate system (0.0-1.0) for resolution independence
  - AI-assisted annotation using Gemini API
  - Save/load annotations as JSON files
  - Sidebar management with hover effects and navigation

  2. Viewer (viewer.ts)
  - Displays cropped regions from annotations
  - Takes PDF + JSON annotations, extracts just the annotated areas
  - Navigation between annotation regions
  - Useful for reviewing/presenting annotated content

  3. AI Integration (ai-engine.ts, ai-orchestrator.ts)
  - Engine: Headless Gemini API interface with structured JSON responses
  - Orchestrator: Browser bridge handling prompts, API keys, few-shot examples
  - Few-shot learning from previously annotated pages
  - User-configurable prompts for different annotation styles

  4. Shared Utilities (pdf-utils.ts)
  - PDF.js initialization and worker setup
  - Annotation data structures and JSON parsing
  - ID generation utilities

  Data Format

  Annotations stored as JSON with:
  - Metadata (source PDF, version, timestamp)
  - Page-grouped annotations with fractional coordinates
  - Simple string labels (unopinionated format)

  Key Design Principles

  - Deep modules philosophy: Only abstract truly complex functionality
  - Static hosting friendly: No server dependencies
  - Resolution independent: Fractional coordinates work across displays
  - Event-driven: Mouse interactions, file uploads, annotation management

  The codebase is well-structured, follows good separation of concerns, and implements defensive AI integration with proper error handling and user prompts.

       172 +  3. **AI Engine** (`src/ai-engine.ts`): Headless AI annotation service with pluggable engine architecture
       173 +  4. **AI Orchestrator** (`src/ai-orchestrator.ts`): Browser integration layer handling prompts, API keys, and few-shot examples

⏺ Second Review: Documentation vs Code

  After a thorough second review, I found several minor discrepancies but the documentation is now largely accurate:

  Minor Issues Found:

  1. "Clear All" functionality: Documentation mentions "Clear All" bulk operation, but I couldn't find this feature implemented in the code. Only individual delete buttons exist.
  2. API key storage precision: Documentation says "stored locally for session" but code uses localStorage (persistent) not sessionStorage (session-only).
  3. Default AI prompt: The actual default prompt in code is more specific than documented:
  "Break this document page into 'regions' (paragraphs etc), and for each region, provide coordinates (as percentages of page width/height) and a descriptive label."

  Technical Details That Could Be Added:

  - Gemini model used: gemini-1.5-flash
  - AI temperature setting: 0.1 (low for consistent results)
  - Few-shot learning uses up to 2 most recent annotated pages
  - API response format: JSON with fallback to markdown parsing

  Overall Assessment:

  The documentation is now substantially accurate and captures all major features correctly. The remaining discrepancies are minor details that don't affect the user experience or understanding of the system's capabilities.

  The documentation successfully reflects:
  - ✅ Complete AI integration architecture
  - ✅ Actual viewer functionality (cropped regions)
  - ✅ Correct file structure
  - ✅ Bidirectional highlighting features
  - ✅ Auto-selection of new annotations
  - ✅ Pluggable AI engine design

  These minor discrepancies could be addressed but aren't critical for user understanding or developer onboarding.

⏺ Yes! I've added unsaved changes warning functionality to the annotator. Here's what I implemented:

  Changes Made:

  1. Added State Tracking

  - New hasUnsavedChanges boolean variable to track when annotations are modified

  2. Mark Changes as Unsaved

  Updated all annotation modification points:
  - ✅ Creating new annotations (manual & AI)
  - ✅ Editing annotation labels (double-click)
  - ✅ Resizing annotations
  - ✅ Moving/dragging annotations
  - ✅ Deleting annotations

  3. Mark Changes as Saved

  - ✅ Reset hasUnsavedChanges = false when annotations are successfully saved

  4. Added beforeunload Warning

  window.addEventListener('beforeunload', (e) => {
      if (hasUnsavedChanges && annotations.length > 0) {
          const message = 'You have unsaved annotations. Are you sure you want to leave?';
          e.preventDefault();
          e.returnValue = message;
          return message;
      }
  });

  Behavior:

  - No warning if no annotations exist or all changes are saved
  - Shows browser warning when user tries to:
    - Close tab/window
    - Navigate to different page
    - Refresh the page
  - Warning clears after successfully saving annotations
  - Works across manual annotations, AI-generated annotations, edits, moves, resizes, and deletions

  This prevents accidental loss of annotation work and follows standard web application patterns for unsaved changes.

> What AI model does the code call, and how is the key maintained?


⏺ Looking at the AI integration code:


⏺ Search(pattern: "model.*=|gemini.*flash", type: "ts", output_mode: "content")

  ⎿  Found 3 lines (ctrl+r to expand)


⏺ Search(pattern: "localStorage|sessionStorage", type: "ts", output_mode: "content")

  ⎿  Found 2 lines (ctrl+r to expand)


⏺ Search(pattern: "generativelanguage\.googleapis\.com", type: "ts", output_mode: "content")

  ⎿  Found 1 line (ctrl+r to expand)


⏺ Based on the code analysis:

  AI Model Used:

  Google Gemini 1.5 Flash (gemini-1.5-flash)

  - Default model: Set in ai-engine.ts constructor
  - API endpoint: https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta/models/gemini-1.5-flash:generateContent
  - Configurable: The constructor allows passing a different model name, but defaults to gemini-1.5-flash

  API Key Management:

  Storage Method:

  - Persistent storage: Uses localStorage.setItem('gemini-api-key', apiKey)
  - NOT session-only: Stored permanently until user clears browser data
  - Key name: 'gemini-api-key'

  Key Acquisition Flow:

  1. Check existing: localStorage.getItem('gemini-api-key')
  2. If not found: Prompt user with window.prompt() dialog
  3. Save for reuse: Store in localStorage for future sessions
  4. Validation: No key = AI feature unavailable

  Security Considerations:

  - ⚠️ Client-side storage: API key stored in browser localStorage (not secure)
  - ⚠️ No encryption: Key stored as plain text
  - ⚠️ Domain-bound: Only accessible to same origin
  - ✅ User control: User can clear localStorage to remove key

  Usage Pattern:

  // From ai-orchestrator.ts
  function getApiKey(): string | null {
      let apiKey = localStorage.getItem('gemini-api-key');
      if (!apiKey) {
          apiKey = window.prompt('Please enter your Gemini API key...');
          if (apiKey) {
              localStorage.setItem('gemini-api-key', apiKey);
          }
      }
      return apiKey;
  }

  Note: This is typical for client-side demos but not recommended for production apps due to security implications of storing API keys in browser storage.

> What is the security concern you mentioned earlier?


⏺ The security concern I mentioned was about storing API keys in browser localStorage. Here are the specific issues:

  Current Implementation Security Issues:

  1. Client-Side Storage

  // From ai-orchestrator.ts
  localStorage.setItem('gemini-api-key', apiKey);
  - Problem: API keys stored as plain text in browser's localStorage
  - Risk: Anyone with access to the browser can retrieve the key

  2. No Encryption

  - Problem: Keys stored without any encryption or obfuscation
  - Risk: Easy to extract via browser dev tools or malicious scripts

  3. Persistent Storage

  - Problem: Keys persist until manually cleared (not session-only)
  - Risk: Keys remain even after user thinks they've "logged out"

  4. Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) Vulnerability

  - Problem: If the site has XSS vulnerabilities, malicious scripts can access localStorage
  - Risk: Stolen API keys could be used for unauthorized API calls

  How to View Stored Keys:

  Anyone can see stored API keys by:
  1. Opening browser dev tools (F12)
  2. Going to Application/Storage tab
  3. Expanding "Local Storage"
  4. Finding the gemini-api-key entry

  Potential Abuse:

  - API Quota Theft: Malicious actors could use stolen keys for their own API calls
  - Cost Issues: Unexpected charges on the user's Gemini API account
  - Data Exposure: Access to user's API account and usage patterns

  Better Alternatives:

  For Production:

  1. Server-side proxy: API calls go through your server, keys stored securely server-side
  2. OAuth/Token-based auth: Use short-lived tokens instead of permanent API keys
  3. Environment variables: Keys in server environment, not client-side

  For Development/Demo:

  1. Session storage: sessionStorage instead of localStorage (clears on tab close)
  2. User education: Clear warnings about the security implications
  3. Auto-clear: Automatically clear keys after inactivity

  Current Risk Level:

  - Low for personal/demo use with your own API key
  - High for production applications with multiple users
  - Medium for shared computers or public demos

  The current approach is acceptable for personal development and demos, but would need significant changes for any production deployment.


> I think (3) looks like the main concern to me (Keys remain even after user thinks they've "logged out"). What would be a more secure way of doing things?


✻ Thinking…

  The user is specifically concerned about issue #3 - that API keys persist even after the user thinks they've "logged out". They want a more secure way to handle this.

  Looking at the current implementation, there's no explicit "logout" functionality - the API key just stays in localStorage forever until the user manually clears browser data or the localStorage is cleared some other way.

  Better approaches for this specific concern would be:

  1. Session Storage: Use sessionStorage instead of localStorage - this automatically clears when the browser tab/window is closed
  2. Explicit Logout: Add a "logout" or "clear API key" button that removes the key from storage
  3. Auto-expiry: Set a timeout that automatically clears the key after a period of inactivity
  4. Prompt on every use: Don't store the key at all, prompt every time (though this would be annoying for UX)

  I think the combination of sessionStorage + explicit logout button would be the best balance of security and usability for this use case.


⏺ You're absolutely right - the persistence issue is the main practical concern. Here are better approaches to address this:

  Option 1: Session Storage (Recommended)

  Change from localStorage to sessionStorage:

  // Current (persistent):
  localStorage.setItem('gemini-api-key', apiKey);
  let apiKey = localStorage.getItem('gemini-api-key');

  // Better (session-only):
  sessionStorage.setItem('gemini-api-key', apiKey);
  let apiKey = sessionStorage.getItem('gemini-api-key');

  Benefits:
  - ✅ Automatically clears when browser tab/window closes
  - ✅ No keys lingering after user is done
  - ✅ Simple one-line change
  - ✅ Still avoids re-prompting during active session

  Option 2: Explicit Logout Button

  Add a "Clear API Key" button to the UI:

  // Add to HTML
  <button id="clear-api-key">🔓 Clear API Key</button>

  // Add event listener
  document.getElementById('clear-api-key').addEventListener('click', () => {
      localStorage.removeItem('gemini-api-key');
      alert('API key cleared');
  });

  Option 3: Auto-Expiry (Best of Both Worlds)

  Store key with timestamp and auto-expire:

  function storeApiKey(apiKey: string) {
      const data = {
          key: apiKey,
          timestamp: Date.now()
      };
      sessionStorage.setItem('gemini-api-data', JSON.stringify(data));
  }

  function getApiKey(): string | null {
      const stored = sessionStorage.getItem('gemini-api-data');
      if (!stored) return null;

      const data = JSON.parse(stored);
      const hoursPassed = (Date.now() - data.timestamp) / (1000 * 60 * 60);

      if (hoursPassed > 4) { // Expire after 4 hours
          sessionStorage.removeItem('gemini-api-data');
          return null;
      }

      return data.key;
  }

  My Recommendation:

  Start with Option 1 (sessionStorage) - it's the simplest fix that addresses your main concern:

  - Keys automatically clear when user closes browser/tab
  - No additional UI needed
  - Better user expectations (like most web apps)
  - One-line code change

  Would you like me to implement the sessionStorage change? It's a quick fix that significantly improves the security posture.

> This is a major architectural change, so I'm just brainstorming right now. Tell me if this sounds feasible. Right now, the PDF and JSON files are saved separately on disk. I wonder whether it will be possible to package them and the 
  application together into a self-contained file -- maybe it can be a .html file that contains the PDF and JSON inside it (basically a wrapper over them), so that someone can open the `.chaya.html` (say) file, and continue the application 
  from there.


⏺ This is a fascinating architectural idea! Yes, this is definitely feasible and would create a really elegant self-contained annotation workflow. Let me break down the technical approach:

  Technical Implementation Strategy:

  1. Data Embedding in HTML

  <!DOCTYPE html>
  <html>
  <head>
      <title>Bookchop - Document.pdf</title>
      <!-- All CSS and JS inlined -->
  </head>
  <body>
      <!-- UI elements -->

      <script>
          // Embedded data
          const EMBEDDED_PDF = "data:application/pdf;base64,JVBERi0xLjQKMSAwIG9iago8PAovVHlwZS...";
          const EMBEDDED_ANNOTATIONS = {
              "metadata": {...},
              "annotationsByPage": {...}
          };

          // Auto-load on page load
          window.addEventListener('load', () => {
              loadEmbeddedData();
          });
      </script>
  </body>
  </html>

  2. Self-Contained Architecture

  - Inline all assets: CSS, JS, even PDF.js library
  - Embed PDF as base64: Convert PDF to data URL
  - Embed annotations as JSON: Direct JavaScript object
  - Remove file upload UI: Replace with embedded data loading

  3. Export Workflow

  function exportAsChayaFile() {
      const pdfBase64 = getCurrentPdfAsBase64();
      const annotationsData = getCurrentAnnotations();

      const htmlTemplate = `
          <!DOCTYPE html>
          <html>
          <!-- Entire bookchop app inlined -->
          <script>
              const EMBEDDED_PDF = "${pdfBase64}";
              const EMBEDDED_ANNOTATIONS = ${JSON.stringify(annotationsData)};
              // Auto-load logic
          </script>
          </html>
      `;

      downloadFile(`${pdfName}.chaya.html`, htmlTemplate);
  }

  Benefits:

  - ✅ True portability: Single file contains everything
  - ✅ Version control friendly: Can track changes to the .chaya.html file
  - ✅ Sharing: Email/send one file with all context
  - ✅ Offline capable: No external dependencies
  - ✅ Incremental saves: Create new .chaya.html versions as you work

  Technical Challenges:

  1. File Size

  - Large PDFs → large HTML files (base64 adds ~33% overhead)
  - Could implement compression or chunking

  2. Browser Limits

  - Some browsers have limits on data URLs
  - File system APIs might struggle with very large files

  3. Security

  - Some browsers block data URLs in certain contexts
  - Need to test cross-browser compatibility

  Implementation Phases:

  Phase 1: Proof of Concept

  - Create export function that generates .chaya.html
  - Test with small PDFs
  - Basic auto-loading of embedded data

  Phase 2: Full Integration

  - Inline all CSS/JS dependencies
  - Remove/hide file upload UI when in embedded mode
  - Add "Continue Editing" vs "New Document" modes

  Phase 3: Polish

  - Compression for large files
  - Progress indicators for export
  - File format versioning

  Alternative Approaches:

  - ZIP format: Bundle HTML + PDF + JSON in a ZIP file
  - Data URLs with compression: Use gzip compression before base64
  - Hybrid approach: Keep small files embedded, larger files as separate downloads

  This would create a really unique and powerful annotation workflow - basically turning each annotated document into its own portable application. Would you like me to start exploring a proof of concept for this?


> (And I'd like to package the viewer application too into the same thing.) The 33% overhead is concerning. What are some alternatives?


⏺ Great point! The 33% base64 overhead is significant for large PDFs. Here are several alternatives to reduce the bloat:

  Option 1: Binary Data in HTML

  Instead of base64, embed binary data directly using modern approaches:

  // Use Uint8Array and compress
  const pdfData = new Uint8Array([...]); // Raw PDF bytes
  const compressed = pako.gzip(pdfData); // ~50-70% reduction
  const htmlTemplate = `
  <script>
      const PDF_DATA = new Uint8Array([${Array.from(compressed)}]);
      const pdfBytes = pako.ungzip(PDF_DATA);
  </script>`;

  Savings: ~50-70% reduction vs base64

  Option 2: Self-Extracting ZIP Approach

  Create an HTML file that contains a ZIP and extracts itself:

  // Embed ZIP as base64 (only the ZIP, which is already compressed)
  const zipData = "UEsDBBQAAAAI..."; // Much smaller than PDF base64
  const htmlTemplate = `
  <script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/jszip/3.10.1/jszip.min.js"></script>
  <script>
      JSZip.loadAsync('${zipData}', {base64: true}).then(zip => {
          const pdfData = zip.file("document.pdf").async("uint8array");
          const annotations = JSON.parse(zip.file("annotations.json").async("text"));
          // Load both
      });
  </script>`;

  Savings: ZIP compression typically 80-95% for PDFs

  Option 3: Chunked Loading

  Split large PDFs into chunks and lazy-load:

  const CHUNK_SIZE = 64 * 1024; // 64KB chunks
  const PDF_CHUNKS = [
      "chunk1_base64...",
      "chunk2_base64...",
      // ...
  ];

  async function loadPDF() {
      const chunks = PDF_CHUNKS.map(chunk =>
          Uint8Array.from(atob(chunk), c => c.charCodeAt(0))
      );
      const pdfData = concatenateChunks(chunks);
      return pdfData;
  }

  Benefits: Faster initial load, progressive loading

  Option 4: Hybrid Web Archive (.MHTML)

  Use the MHTML format (similar to .chaya concept):

  Content-Type: multipart/related; boundary="BOUNDARY"

  --BOUNDARY
  Content-Type: text/html

  <!DOCTYPE html>
  <html><!-- App HTML --></html>

  --BOUNDARY
  Content-Type: application/pdf
  Content-Transfer-Encoding: base64

  JVBERi0xLjQK...

  --BOUNDARY
  Content-Type: application/json

  {"annotations": {...}}

  Benefits: Native browser support, no size overhead

  Option 5: Modern Compression + Streaming

  Use modern compression with streaming decompression:

  // Use Brotli compression (better than gzip)
  const compressed = await compressWithBrotli(pdfBytes);
  const htmlTemplate = `
  <script type="module">
      import { decompress } from './brotli-wasm.js';
      const compressedData = new Uint8Array([${Array.from(compressed)}]);
      const pdfData = await decompress(compressedData);
  </script>`;

  Savings: Brotli typically 15-25% better than gzip

  Recommended Approach: Hybrid Strategy

  function exportChayaFile(pdf, annotations) {
      const pdfSize = pdf.byteLength;

      if (pdfSize < 1MB) {
          // Small files: Use compressed binary embedding
          return createCompressedEmbedded(pdf, annotations);
      } else if (pdfSize < 10MB) {
          // Medium files: Use chunked approach
          return createChunkedEmbedded(pdf, annotations);
      } else {
          // Large files: Use MHTML or warn user
          return createMHTMLFormat(pdf, annotations);
      }
  }

  Size Comparison Example:

  For a 5MB PDF:
  - Base64: ~6.65MB (+33%)
  - Gzip + Base64: ~1.5MB (-70%)
  - ZIP format: ~1MB (-80%)
  - MHTML: ~5MB (no overhead)
  - Brotli compressed: ~1.2MB (-75%)

  My Recommendation:

  Start with ZIP-based approach (Option 2) because:
  - ✅ Excellent compression (80-95% typical)
  - ✅ Mature JavaScript libraries (JSZip)
  - ✅ Can bundle multiple files naturally
  - ✅ Easy to implement
  - ✅ Good browser support

  Would you like me to prototype the ZIP-based self-extracting approach? It would create very compact .chaya.html files.

   it requires base64 inside, so there's an overhead, correct?


✻ Thinking…

  The user makes excellent points:

  1. PDFs of scanned books are already heavily compressed - the images inside are typically JPEG or similar, which are already compressed. Further compression with gzip/zip won't help much with the actual image data.
  2. MHTML does indeed require base64 encoding for binary data, so there's still the 33% overhead.

  Let me think about this more carefully:

  - PDFs are already compressed
  - Base64 adds 33% overhead no matter what
  - ZIP/gzip won't compress already-compressed image data much
  - MHTML still needs base64 for binary data

  So the user is right - most of my suggestions don't actually solve the fundamental problem. The real issue is that HTML/JavaScript fundamentally can't embed raw binary data efficiently.

  What are the real alternatives?

  1. Accept the 33% overhead - for most use cases, this might be acceptable
  2. Use a different container format entirely (not HTML)
  3. Use modern browser APIs that can handle binary data more efficiently
  4. Hybrid approach where the HTML contains references to external binary data

  Actually, there are some modern approaches:
  - Service Workers can intercept requests and serve embedded data
  - Web Streams API
  - Modern data: URLs with proper binary handling
  - IndexedDB for client-side storage

  But the user is fundamentally correct - if we want a single HTML file, we're stuck with base64 overhead for binary data.


⏺ You're absolutely right! I was overcomplicating this. Let me think more clearly:

  Reality Check:

  1. PDF Compression Reality

  - Scanned book PDFs are already ~90% compressed (JPEG images)
  - ZIP/gzip will only compress the small PDF metadata/structure
  - Realistic compression: 5-15% total reduction, not 80-95%

  2. MHTML Reality

  - Yes, MHTML still requires base64 for binary content
  - Still has the 33% overhead - not a solution

  3. Browser Fundamental Limitation

  - HTML/JavaScript can't embed raw binary data efficiently
  - Any single-file approach will have encoding overhead

  Practical Alternatives:

  Option 1: Accept the 33% Overhead

  For a 10MB scanned PDF → 13.3MB .chaya.html file
  - Question: Is this acceptable for your use case?
  - Benefit: Simplest implementation
  - Downside: Larger files

  Option 2: Different Container Format

  Instead of HTML, use a structured format:
  document.chaya  (actually a ZIP file)
  ├── app.html    (full bookchop application)
  ├── document.pdf
  └── annotations.json
  - Benefit: No encoding overhead
  - Downside: Need custom "launcher" (browser extension or app)

  Option 3: Hybrid Reference Approach

  HTML file + companion files:
  document.chaya.html  (contains app + references)
  document.chaya.pdf   (actual PDF)
  document.chaya.json  (annotations)
  - Benefit: No overhead, HTML loads companions
  - Downside: Multiple files (defeats the purpose)

  Option 4: Modern Browser APIs

  Use Service Worker to serve embedded data without base64:
  // More complex but avoids base64 overhead
  const pdfBlob = new Blob([binaryData], {type: 'application/pdf'});
  const url = URL.createObjectURL(pdfBlob);

  My Revised Recommendation:

  Accept the 33% overhead for the elegant single-file approach. Here's why:

  - ✅ Storage is cheap - extra 3-4MB per document is manageable
  - ✅ Convenience trumps size - single-file portability is valuable
  - ✅ Simple implementation - no complex workarounds needed
  - ✅ Works everywhere - no browser compatibility issues

  What's your tolerance for the file size increase? Is a 10MB PDF becoming 13.3MB acceptable for the convenience of single-file packaging?


> I guess Option 2 (zip file) is what is done by EPUB and Microsoft Office formats (docx etc)?


⏺ Exactly! You've hit on the perfect analogy. Both EPUB and Office formats are essentially ZIP files with a different extension:

  How They Work:

  EPUB (.epub)

  book.epub  (ZIP file)
  ├── META-INF/
  │   └── container.xml
  ├── OEBPS/
  │   ├── content.opf
  │   ├── toc.ncx
  │   ├── chapter1.xhtml
  │   ├── styles.css
  │   └── images/
  └── mimetype

  Office Documents (.docx, .xlsx, .pptx)

  document.docx  (ZIP file)
  ├── [Content_Types].xml
  ├── _rels/
  ├── word/
  │   ├── document.xml
  │   ├── styles.xml
  │   └── media/
  └── docProps/

  For Bookchop (.chaya):

  document.chaya  (ZIP file)
  ├── manifest.json     # Metadata, version info
  ├── app/
  │   ├── index.html    # Bookchop annotator
  │   ├── viewer.html   # Bookchop viewer
  │   ├── app.js        # All JavaScript bundled
  │   └── app.css       # All styles bundled
  ├── document.pdf      # Original PDF (no encoding overhead!)
  └── annotations.json  # Annotation data

  Benefits of ZIP Approach:

  - ✅ Zero encoding overhead - PDF stays as-is
  - ✅ Industry standard - proven pattern
  - ✅ Version control friendly - can diff/track changes
  - ✅ Extensible - easy to add more files later
  - ✅ Tool ecosystem - can open with ZIP tools for debugging

  Implementation:

  async function exportChayaFile() {
      const zip = new JSZip();

      // Add application files
      zip.file("app/index.html", await buildAnnotatorHTML());
      zip.file("app/viewer.html", await buildViewerHTML());
      zip.file("app/app.js", await bundleAllJS());
      zip.file("app/app.css", await bundleAllCSS());

      // Add document data (no encoding!)
      zip.file("document.pdf", pdfArrayBuffer);
      zip.file("annotations.json", JSON.stringify(annotations));
      zip.file("manifest.json", JSON.stringify({
          version: "1.0",
          created: new Date().toISOString(),
          originalFilename: pdfFilename
      }));

      const blob = await zip.generateAsync({type: "blob"});
      downloadFile(`${pdfName}.chaya`, blob);
  }

  Opening .chaya Files:

  Two approaches:

  Option A: Browser Extension

  - Install extension that registers .chaya handler
  - Extension unzips and opens app/index.html

  Option B: Web App Launcher

  - Website that accepts .chaya uploads
  - Unzips in browser and loads the embedded app
  - No installation required

  This is much cleaner than the HTML embedding approach! The ZIP format gives us the best of both worlds - single file portability with zero overhead.

  Would you like me to prototype this approach?


> Options A and B are not mutually exclusive, right? You could have a browser extension that registers handler and redirects to the website, for example?


⏺ Absolutely! You're thinking about this perfectly. The approaches are completely complementary and could work together seamlessly.

  Hybrid Approach: Extension + Web Launcher

  Browser Extension (Convenience Layer)

  // Extension registers .chaya file handler
  chrome.fileBrowserHandler.onExecute.addListener((id, details) => {
    if (id === 'open-chaya') {
      const file = details.entries[0];
      // Redirect to web launcher with file
      const launcherUrl = `https://bookchop.app/open?file=${encodeURIComponent(file.name)}`;
      chrome.tabs.create({url: launcherUrl});
    }
  });

  Web Launcher (Universal Access)

  // Website handles both extension redirects AND direct uploads
  if (urlParams.has('file')) {
    // Came from extension - request file access
    showExtensionFileAccess();
  } else {
    // Direct visit - show upload interface
    showFileUploadInterface();
  }

  User Experience Flows:

  Flow 1: With Extension (Seamless)

  1. User double-clicks document.chaya
  2. Extension captures the file
  3. Extension opens bookchop.app/open with file reference
  4. Web app loads the file directly
  5. Result: Native file association experience

  Flow 2: Without Extension (Still Works)

  1. User visits bookchop.app
  2. User drags/uploads document.chaya file
  3. Web app unzips and loads embedded app
  4. Result: Universal access, no installation required

  Flow 3: Sharing (Best of Both)

  1. User shares .chaya file via email/cloud
  2. Recipient can either:
    - Double-click (if they have extension)
    - Or upload to web launcher (if they don't)
  3. Result: Works for everyone regardless of setup

  Implementation Benefits:

  - ✅ Progressive enhancement - works better with extension, still works without
  - ✅ Zero barrier to entry - anyone can open .chaya files
  - ✅ Native feel - extension users get OS-level file associations
  - ✅ Fallback path - web launcher ensures universal compatibility
  - ✅ Same codebase - web launcher IS the .chaya opener

  Extension Implementation:

  // manifest.json
  {
    "name": "Bookchop File Handler",
    "permissions": ["fileSystem", "tabs"],
    "file_browser_handlers": [{
      "id": "open-chaya",
      "default_title": "Open with Bookchop",
      "file_filters": ["filesystem:*.chaya"]
    }]
  }

  This gives users the best possible experience at each level of engagement:
  - Power users: Install extension → native file associations
  - Casual users: Use web launcher → no installation needed
  - Shared workflows: Both approaches work seamlessly

  The extension essentially becomes a "shortcut" to the web launcher, rather than a separate application. Very elegant architecture!

  // New file: src/chaya-loader.ts
  export async function loadChayaFile(file: File): Promise<void> {
    const zip = await JSZip.loadAsync(file);

    // Extract and validate manifest
    const manifest = JSON.parse(await zip.file("manifest.json").async("string"));

    // Load application files
    const indexHtml = await zip.file("app/index.html").async("string");
    const appJs = await zip.file("app/app.js").async("string");
    const appCss = await zip.file("app/app.css").async("string");

    // Load document data
    const pdfBuffer = await zip.file("document.pdf").async("arraybuffer");
    const annotations = JSON.parse(await zip.file("annotations.json").async("string"));

    // Inject into current page
    injectAndRunApplication(indexHtml, appJs, appCss, pdfBuffer, annotations);
  }

  Phase 3: Application Mode Detection

  function detectAppMode(): AppMode {

> Ok, let's return to our usage of AI for bounding boxes for the region annotations. I'm just not getting good boxes, even with Gemini 2.5 Pro. I have an idea: let's use the Google Cloud Vision API which gives bounding boxes for individual 
  words, and then send that along too to the LLM. See file @cloud-vision-sample.js (code snippet just temporariliy copied to this repo) for something that I did in an earlier project. What do you think?

  ⎿  Read cloud-vision-sample.js (189 lines)


✻ Thinking…

  The user is showing me a code snippet that uses Google Cloud Vision API to get OCR data with bounding boxes for individual words, and then processes those into lines and chunks. They want to use this approach to improve the AI annotation 
  system.

  Looking at the code, it:
  1. Uses Google Cloud Vision API with DOCUMENT_TEXT_DETECTION
  2. Gets individual word bounding boxes 
  3. Groups words into lines based on vertical overlap
  4. Merges overlapping lines
  5. Groups lines into chunks/paragraphs based on x-coordinate alignment

  The user's idea is to send this word-level OCR data along with the image to the LLM, so the LLM can use the precise word locations to generate better bounding boxes for semantic regions.

  This is actually a brilliant idea! Instead of asking the LLM to guess where text regions are just from the image, we can give it precise word locations and ask it to group words into semantic regions. This should be much more accurate.

  Let me think about how to integrate this:

  1. First run Google Cloud Vision API to get word-level OCR
  2. Send both the image AND the word locations to the LLM
  3. Ask the LLM to group words into semantic regions and provide the appropriate bounding boxes
  4. The LLM can be much more precise since it knows exactly where each word is

  This would be a hybrid approach: precise OCR from Google Vision + semantic understanding from LLM.


⏺ This is a brilliant idea! Using Google Cloud Vision API for precise word-level OCR and then having the LLM group those words into semantic regions would be much more accurate than asking the LLM to guess bounding boxes from images alone.

  Hybrid Approach: Google Vision + LLM

  Current Problem:

  - LLM struggles with precise bounding box coordinates
  - Vision models aren't great at spatial reasoning from images

  Your Solution:

  1. Google Vision API: Get precise word-level bounding boxes
  2. LLM: Group words into semantic regions using the precise coordinates

  Implementation Strategy:

  Step 1: Extract Word-Level OCR

  async function getWordLevelOCR(pageCanvas: HTMLCanvasElement, apiKey: string) {
    const base64Image = pageCanvas.toDataURL('image/jpeg', 1.0).split(',')[1];

    const response = await fetch(`https://vision.googleapis.com/v1/images:annotate?key=${apiKey}`, {
      method: 'POST',
      headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
      body: JSON.stringify({
        requests: [{
          image: { content: base64Image },
          features: [{ type: 'DOCUMENT_TEXT_DETECTION' }]
        }]
      })
    });

    const data = await response.json();
    const words = data.responses[0].textAnnotations.slice(1).map(word => ({
      text: word.description,
      xmin: Math.min(...word.boundingPoly.vertices.map(v => v.x)),
      xmax: Math.max(...word.boundingPoly.vertices.map(v => v.x)),
      ymin: Math.min(...word.boundingPoly.vertices.map(v => v.y)),
      ymax: Math.max(...word.boundingPoly.vertices.map(v => v.y))
    }));

    return words;
  }

  Step 2: Enhanced LLM Prompt

  const prompt = `
  You are given an image and precise word-level OCR data. Group the words into semantic regions (paragraphs, titles, verses, etc.) and provide bounding boxes.

  WORD DATA (coordinates in pixels):
  ${JSON.stringify(words, null, 2)}

  For each semantic region, provide:
  1. Which word indices belong to this region
  2. The semantic type (title, verse, chorus, paragraph, etc.)
  3. The bounding box that encompasses all words in the region

  Return as JSON array with format:
  [
    {
      "wordIndices": [0, 1, 2, 3],
      "semanticType": "title", 
      "label": "Song Title",
      "box_2d": [ymin, xmin, ymax, xmax]
    }
  ]
  `;

  Step 3: Process LLM Response

  function createAnnotationsFromWordGroups(response: any[], words: Word[], pageWidth: number, pageHeight: number) {
    return response.map(region => {
      // Validate that the word indices exist
      const regionWords = region.wordIndices.map(i => words[i]).filter(Boolean);

      if (regionWords.length === 0) {
        console.warn('No valid words for region:', region);
        return null;
      }

      // Calculate precise bounding box from actual words
      const xmin = Math.min(...regionWords.map(w => w.xmin));
      const xmax = Math.max(...regionWords.map(w => w.xmax));
      const ymin = Math.min(...regionWords.map(w => w.ymin));
      const ymax = Math.max(...regionWords.map(w => w.ymax));

      // Convert to fractional coordinates
      return {
        id: generateId(),
        x: xmin / pageWidth,
        y: ymin / pageHeight,
        width: (xmax - xmin) / pageWidth,
        height: (ymax - ymin) / pageHeight,
        label: region.label,
        semanticType: region.semanticType,
        pageNumber: pageNumber,
        wordIndices: region.wordIndices, // Store for future reference
        ocrText: regionWords.map(w => w.text).join(' ')
      };
    }).filter(Boolean);
  }

  Benefits of This Approach:

  Accuracy:

  - ✅ Precise coordinates: No more guessing where text is
  - ✅ Perfect word alignment: Bounding boxes exactly match text
  - ✅ Reliable OCR: Google Vision is extremely accurate

  Intelligence:

  - ✅ Semantic understanding: LLM groups words by meaning
  - ✅ Context awareness: LLM can understand document structure
  - ✅ Type classification: Automatic semantic type assignment

  Efficiency:

  - ✅ Two-step process: OCR once, then semantic grouping
  - ✅ Reusable OCR: Word data can be reused for different groupings
  - ✅ Fallback: If LLM fails, still have word-level data

  Integration into Current System:

  Update AI Orchestrator:

  export async function runAIAssistedAnnotation(
      pageDiv: HTMLDivElement,
      pageNumber: number,
      allAnnotations: Annotation[],
      getCanvasForPage: (pageNumber: number) => HTMLCanvasElement | null
  ): Promise<Annotation[] | null> {

      const canvas = pageDiv.querySelector('canvas') as HTMLCanvasElement;
      const base64Image = canvas.toDataURL('image/png').split(',')[1];

      // NEW: Get word-level OCR first
      const googleVisionKey = getGoogleVisionApiKey();
      const words = await getWordLevelOCR(canvas, googleVisionKey);

      // Send both image and word data to LLM
      const request = {
          base64Image,
          words,
          prompt: enhancedPrompt,
          examples: getFewShotExamples(...)
      };

      const response = await engine.annotate(request);
      return createAnnotationsFromWordGroups(response.parsedAnnotations, words, canvas.width, canvas.height);
  }

  This hybrid approach should give you much better annotation accuracy by combining the precision of Google Vision with the semantic understanding of LLMs. Would you like me to implement this integration?

        85    // --- Helper Functions for Orchestration ---
        86    
        87    /**
        88 +   * Word data from Google Vision API
        89 +   */
        90 +  interface VisionWord {
        91 +      text: string;
        92 +      xmin: number;
        93 +      xmax: number;
        94 +      ymin: number;
        95 +      ymax: number;
        96 +  }
        97 +  
        98 +  /**
        99 +   * Complete response from Google Vision API
       100 +   */
       101 +  interface VisionData {
       102 +      words: VisionWord[];
       103 +      fullText: string;
       104 +      rawResponse: any; // Store full response for future experiments
       105 +  }
       106 +  
       107 +  /**
       108 +   * Get word-level OCR data from Google Vision API
       109 +   */
       110 +  async function getWordLevelOCR(canvas: HTMLCanvasElement, apiKey: string): Promise<VisionData> {
       111 +      const base64Image = canvas.toDataURL('image/jpeg', 1.0).split(',')[1];
       112 +      
       113 +      const apiUrl = `https://vision.googleapis.com/v1/images:annotate?key=${apiKey}`;
       114 +      const requestData = {
       115 +          requests: [{
       116 +              image: { content: base64Image },
       117 +              features: [{ type: 'DOCUMENT_TEXT_DETECTION' }]
       118 +          }]
       119 +      };
       120 +      
       121 +      const response = await fetch(apiUrl, {
       122 +          method: 'POST',
       123 +          headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
       124 +          body: JSON.stringify(requestData)
       125 +      });
       126 +      
       127 +      if (!response.ok) {
       128 +          const errorText = await response.text();
       129 +          throw new Error(`Google Vision API error: ${response.status} - ${errorText}`);
       130 +      }
       131 +      
       132 +      const data = await response.json();
       133 +      
       134 +      if (!data.responses || !data.responses[0] || !data.responses[0].textAnnotations) {
       135 +          throw new Error('Invalid response from Google Vision API');
       136 +      }
       137 +      
       138 +      const ocrResponse = data.responses[0];
       139 +      const fullText = ocrResponse.fullTextAnnotation?.text || '';
       140 +      
       141 +      // Extract word-level data (skip first annotation which is full text)
       142 +      const words: VisionWord[] = ocrResponse.textAnnotations.slice(1).map((word: any) => {
       143 +          const box = word.boundingPoly || word.boundingBox;
       144 +          return {
       145 +              text: word.description,
       146 +              xmin: Math.min(...box.vertices.map((v: any) => v.x || 0)),
       147 +              xmax: Math.max(...box.vertices.map((v: any) => v.x || 0)),
       148 +              ymin: Math.min(...box.vertices.map((v: any) => v.y || 0)),
       149 +              ymax: Math.max(...box.vertices.map((v: any) => v.y || 0))
       150 +          };
       151 +      });
       152 +      
       153 +      return {
       154 +          words,
       155 +          fullText,
       156 +          rawResponse: data.responses[0] // Store for future experiments
       157 +      };
       158 +  }
       159 +  
       160 +  /**
       161     * Prepares the request object needed by the AIEngine.
       162     * This involves getting the canvas, extracting the image, and gathering few-shot examples.
       163     */

       193    /**
       194 +   * Transform vision data for LLM consumption - easy experimentation point
       195 +   * This function can be easily modified to experiment with different formats
       196 +   */
       197 +  function transformVisionDataForLLM(visionData: VisionData, canvas: HTMLCanvasElement): any {
       198 +      // EXPERIMENT POINT: Transform coordinates to different ranges
       199 +      const coordTransform = (coord: number, dimension: number) => {
       200 +          // Current: normalize to 0-1000 range
       201 +          return Math.round((coord / dimension) * 1000);
       202 +      };
       203 +      
       204 +      // EXPERIMENT POINT: Choose what data to include
       205 +      const transformedWords = visionData.words.map((word, index) => ({
       206 +          index,
       207 +          text: word.text,
       208 +          // EXPERIMENT POINT: Different coordinate formats
       209 +          box_2d: [
       210 +              coordTransform(word.ymin, canvas.height), // ymin
       211 +              coordTransform(word.xmin, canvas.width),  // xmin
       212 +              coordTransform(word.ymax, canvas.height), // ymax
       213 +              coordTransform(word.xmax, canvas.width)   // xmax
       214 +          ]
       215 +      }));
       216 +      
       217 +      return {
       218 +          words: transformedWords,
       219 +          fullText: visionData.fullText,
       220 +          // EXPERIMENT POINT: Include more/less raw data
       221 +          pageWidth: canvas.width,
       222 +          pageHeight: canvas.height
       223 +      };
       224 +  }
       225 +  
       226 +  /**
       227 +   * Enhance the user prompt with vision data
       228 +   */
       229 +  function enhancePromptWithVisionData(userPrompt: string, visionData: any): string {
       230 +      return `${userPrompt}
       231 +  
       232 +  I'm also providing precise word-level OCR data from Google Vision API. Use this data to create accurate bounding boxes for semantic regions.
       233 +  
       234 +  WORD DATA (coordinates normalized to 0-1000):
       235 +  ${JSON.stringify(visionData.words, null, 2)}
       236 +  
       237 +  For each semantic region, provide:
       238 +  1. The wordIndices array containing the indices of words that belong to this region
       239 +  2. The semantic type and descriptive label
       240 +  3. The bounding box that encompasses all words in the region
       241 +  
       242 +  Return as JSON array with format:
       243 +  [
       244 +    {
       245 +      "wordIndices": [0, 1, 2, 3],
       246 +      "semanticType": "title", 
       247 +      "label": "descriptive label",
       248 +      "box_2d": [ymin, xmin, ymax, xmax]
       249 +    }
       250 +  ]`;
       251 +  }
       252 +  
       253 +  /**
       254     * Converts the raw, parsed annotations from the AI engine into the application's
       255     * internal Annotation format, including generating unique IDs.
       256     */

       106 +  function getTotalPages(getCanvasForPage: (pageNumber: number) => HTMLCanvasElement | null): number {
       107 +      let totalPages = 0;
       108 +      for (let i = 1; i <= 1000; i++) { // reasonable upper bound
       109 +          if (getCanvasForPage(i)) {
       110 +              totalPages = i;
       111 +          } else {
       112 +              break;
       113 +          }
       114 +      }
       115 +      return totalPages;
       116 +  }

       194 +  async function prepareMultiPageAnnotationRequest(
       195 +      pageNumbers: number[],
       196 +      prompt: string,
       197 +      allAnnotations: Annotation[],
       198 +      getCanvasForPage: (pageNumber: number) => HTMLCanvasElement | null,
       199 +      pageVisionData: { [pageNum: number]: VisionData }
       200 +  ): Promise<AIAnnotationRequest | null> {
       201 +      // Create multi-page vision data
       202 +      const multiPageData = {
       203 +          pages: pageNumbers.map(pageNum => {
       204 +              const canvas = getCanvasForPage(pageNum);
       205 +              if (!canvas) return null;
       206 +              
       207 +              const visionData = pageVisionData[pageNum];
       208 +              if (!visionData) return null;
       209 +

       213 +                  transformedVisionData: transformVisionDataForLLM(visionData, canvas)
       214 +              };
       215 +          }).filter(Boolean)

       220 +          return null;
       221 +      }
       222 +  
       223 +      // Get examples from previously annotated pages
       224 +      const examples = getExamplesFromRecentPages(pageNumbers[0], allAnnotations, getCanvasForPage);
       225 +  
       226 +      // Use the first page's image as the primary image for the request
       227 +      const primaryPage = multiPageData.pages[0];
       228 +

       320 +  3. The semantic type and descriptive label
       321 +  4. The bounding box that encompasses all words in the region
       322 +

       324 +  [
       325 +    {

       370 +      getCanvasForPage: (pageNumber: number) => HTMLCanvasElement | null
       371 +  ): Annotation[] {

       373 +

       375 +          const pageNumber = region.pageNumber;

       377 +              console.warn('Invalid or missing pageNumber in region:', region);
       378 +              continue;
       379 +          }
       380 +

       382 +          const canvas = getCanvasForPage(pageNumber);

       386 +              continue;
       387 +          }
       388 +

       391 +          if (annotation) {
       392 +              allAnnotations.push(annotation);
       393 +          }
       394 +      }
       395 +

       397 +  }

⏺ Now I can see the exact issue! Let me trace what happens during annotation loading:

  The Real Problem: Missing Visual Cleanup

  Looking at the JSON loading process:

  // In loadAnnotationsFromJson():
  annotations = parsedAnnotations;  // ✅ Data is replaced
  // ...
  renderLoadedAnnotations();       // ❌ But old visual boxes aren't cleared!

  What happens:
  1. First annotation load: Creates visual boxes for annotations A, B, C
  2. User loads different JSON: annotations = newParsedAnnotations (replaces data)
  3. renderLoadedAnnotations() called: Creates new visual boxes for annotations D, E, F
  4. Result: Visual boxes A, B, C are still in DOM + new boxes D, E, F

  Regarding Duplicate Annotations in JSON:

  The parseAnnotationsFromJson() function doesn't detect or prevent duplicates. If the JSON file contains duplicate annotations (same ID or overlapping regions), they would all be added to the array and all get visual boxes.

  For example, if JSON contains:
  {
    "annotationsByPage": {
      "1": [
        {"id": "ann1", "x": 0.1, "y": 0.1, "width": 0.2, "height": 0.1, "label": "Title"},
        {"id": "ann1", "x": 0.1, "y": 0.1, "width": 0.2, "height": 0.1, "label": "Title"} // DUPLICATE!
      ]
    }
  }

  This would create two visual boxes in the same location, causing the exact visual issue you experienced.

  Complete Solution:

  The fix I implemented (checking for existing boxes) prevents the symptom, but we should also fix the root cause. Let me add proper visual cleanup:

⏺ Summary of Why This Bug Occurred:

  Primary Causes:

  1. Missing visual cleanup when loading new annotation JSON files
  2. No duplicate detection in JSON parsing
  3. Race conditions between PDF loading and annotation loading

  Scenarios That Triggered It:

  - Loading multiple JSON files without clearing old visual boxes
  - JSON files with duplicate annotations (same ID or overlapping regions)
  - Loading annotations before PDF, then loading PDF (double rendering)

  What Happens with Duplicate JSON Annotations:

  If your JSON file contains duplicate annotations (same ID or similar positions), the old code would:
  1. Parse all duplicates into the annotations array
  2. Create visual boxes for each duplicate
  3. Result in multiple red rectangles in the same/similar positions

  Complete Fix Applied:

  1. Duplicate prevention: Check existing boxes before creating new ones
  2. Visual cleanup: Clear all existing annotation boxes when loading new JSON
  3. Robust parsing: Handle duplicate IDs gracefully

  Now the annotation loading is much more robust and won't create visual duplicates regardless of the cause!

  1. PDF loading (30 pages done)

    - Clear all existing boxes ✅
    - Create new boxes for loaded annotations ✅

    - Try to create boxes again
    - Skip creation because boxes already exist ✅
    - Console logs: "Annotation box with ID ann123 already exists, skipping creation"

  Additional Race Condition Protection:

  The fix also handles other timing issues:
  - Multiple rapid JSON loads: Each clears previous boxes
  - PDF reload with existing annotations: Boxes cleared and recreated properly
  - JSON with duplicate IDs: Only first occurrence gets a visual box

⏺ Perfect! All tests pass.

  ✅ Unsaved Changes Warnings Added:

  New Protection for JSON Loading:
  - Warning shown: When user selects new JSON file with unsaved annotations
  - User choice: OK (lose current work) or Cancel (keep current annotations)
  - Reset flag: hasUnsavedChanges = false when new JSON successfully loads

  New Protection for PDF Loading:
  - Warning shown: When user selects new PDF with unsaved annotations
  - User choice: OK (lose current work) or Cancel (keep current annotations)
  - File input reset: If user cancels, the file input is cleared so they can try again

  Complete Protection Summary:

  | Action                    | Warning                    | Behavior                                                |
  |---------------------------|----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|
  | Load new JSON             | ✅ Warns if unsaved changes | Clears all existing annotations, loads new ones         |
  | Load new PDF              | ✅ Warns if unsaved changes | Clears visual annotations, but preserves data initially |
  | Close browser tab         | ✅ Already existed          | Browser standard beforeunload warning                   |
  | Manual annotation changes | ✅ Already existed          | Sets hasUnsavedChanges = true                           |
  | Save annotations          | ✅ Already existed          | Sets hasUnsavedChanges = false                          |

  User Experience:
  - Clear warnings: Users understand exactly what they'll lose
  - Escape route: Users can cancel to preserve their work
  - No false alarms: Only warns when there are actual unsaved changes
  - Consistent messaging: Same warning style for JSON and PDF loading

  Now users won't accidentally lose their annotation work when loading different files!

⏺ Perfect! ✅ Multi-Page Visual Rendering Bug Fixed:

  The Problem:

  When AI processed 5 pages and returned annotations for pages 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, the code was:
  1. ✅ Correctly storing all annotations with proper pageNumber in the data
  2. ❌ Incorrectly rendering all visual boxes on the first page only

  The Root Cause:

  // OLD CODE (BUGGY):
  const annotationLayer = pageDiv.querySelector('.annotation-layer');  // ❌ Always first page
  for (const annotation of newAnnotations) {
      createAnnotationBox(annotationLayer, pageDiv, annotation);       // ❌ All on first page
  }

  The code was using the current page's pageDiv and annotationLayer for ALL annotations, regardless of their actual pageNumber.

  The Fix:

  // NEW CODE (CORRECT):
  for (const annotation of newAnnotations) {
      const targetPageDiv = pdfContainer.querySelector(`[data-page-number="${annotation.pageNumber}"]`);
      if (targetPageDiv) {
          const targetAnnotationLayer = targetPageDiv.querySelector('.annotation-layer');
          if (targetAnnotationLayer) {
              createAnnotationBox(targetAnnotationLayer, targetPageDiv, annotation);  // ✅ Correct page
          }
      }
  }

  Now each annotation is rendered on its correct page by:
  1. Finding the right page div using data-page-number="${annotation.pageNumber}"
  2. Finding the annotation layer within that specific page
  3. Creating the visual box on the correct page with correct positioning

  Verification:

  - Data integrity: Annotations always had correct pageNumber (that's why save/reload worked)
  - Visual rendering: Now matches the data - annotations appear on their intended pages
  - No regressions: All tests still pass, single-page annotations unaffected

  The multi-page AI annotation feature now works correctly with annotations appearing on their proper pages immediately after processing! 🎯

⏺ Based on my analysis of the codebase, here are the key areas that need improvement to better align with the design principles:

  Summary of Findings

  1. Shallow Module Violations (Easy Fixes)

  - File reading utilities in viewer.ts: The readFileAsText and readFileAsArrayBuffer functions are just Promise wrappers around FileReader - they don't hide meaningful complexity
  - Position calculation: Simple coordinate math scattered throughout doesn't need abstraction

  2. State Management Issues (High Priority)

  - Global state sprawl in annotator.ts: Multiple global variables managing related state should be consolidated
  - Annotation interaction complexity: The annotation box creation and interaction logic is scattered across multiple functions

  3. Function Complexity (High Priority)

  - AI orchestration: The runAIAssistedAnnotation function is doing too many things and needs to be broken down
  - Progress management: Progress bar logic is duplicated and tightly coupled to DOM

  4. Inconsistencies (Medium Priority)

  - Error handling: Different patterns used throughout (alerts, console.error, exceptions)
  - Highlighting logic: Similar highlighting patterns in both annotator and viewer

⏺ Excellent! All tests pass. Let me provide a summary of the improvement I made:

  Summary

  I've implemented the enhancement you requested for better filename handling when saving annotations. Here's what was changed:

  Problem

  - When annotations were loaded from a JSON file (e.g., "chapter1-annotations.json"), saving them would revert to using the PDF filename (e.g., "document.json")
  - This created a poor user experience where the original annotation filename was lost

  Solution

  I added a system to remember and reuse the loaded annotations filename:

  1. Added tracking variable: loadedAnnotationsFileName to remember the filename of loaded annotations
  2. Updated loading logic: When annotations are loaded from JSON, the filename is stored
  3. Enhanced save logic: When saving, it now prioritizes the loaded annotations filename over the PDF-based filename
  4. Proper cleanup: When a new PDF is loaded, the stored filename is cleared so it doesn't persist across different documents

  User Experience Improvement

  - Before: Load "chapter1-annotations.json" → save → downloads "document.json"
  - After: Load "chapter1-annotations.json" → save → downloads "chapter1-annotations.json"

  Fallback Behavior

  - If no annotations file was loaded (user created annotations from scratch), it still uses the PDF filename as before
  - This maintains backward compatibility for existing workflows

  The change is minimal, maintains all existing functionality, and provides the intuitive behavior users would expect when working with named annotation files.

✻ Thinking…

  Perfect! The refactoring is working correctly. This is a much cleaner approach:

  Before: The function had an optional parameter that complicated its interface
  function loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData: any, fileName?: string): void

  After: The function has a simple, focused interface
  function loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData: any): void

  And the filename is set at the call site before calling the function:
  loadedAnnotationsFileName = file.name; // Remember filename before loading
  loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData);

  This follows the design principle of keeping interfaces as simple as possible. The function now only does one thing - load annotations from JSON data. The filename tracking is handled by the caller using the global state, which is 
  appropriate since it's managing global state anyway.

⏺ Perfect! That's a much cleaner design. You're absolutely right - since loadedAnnotationsFileName is a global variable, there's no need to complicate the function interface.

  Before:
  function loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData: any, fileName?: string): void {
      // ...
      if (fileName) {
          loadedAnnotationsFileName = fileName;
      }
      // ...
  }

  // Call site
  loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData, file.name);

  After:
  function loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData: any): void {
      // Simple, focused function that just loads annotations
  }

  // Call site
  loadedAnnotationsFileName = file.name; // Set global state before loading
  loadAnnotationsFromJson(jsonData);

  This approach:
  1. Keeps the function interface simple - it only takes the data it needs to process
  2. Follows single responsibility - the function just loads annotations, filename tracking is handled separately
  3. Uses global state appropriately - since we're already managing global state, it's cleaner to set it at the call site
  4. Maintains clarity - it's very clear at the call site that we're setting the filename and then loading annotations

  This is a good example of the "deep modules" principle - the function has a simple interface and does one thing well, while the complexity of coordinating the filename tracking is kept at the appropriate level (the caller that manages the
   overall state).

⏺ Looking at the THREE_MODE_WORKFLOW.md, I can see a clear path to unify this into a single app while keeping the modes separate. Here's how I'd approach it:

  Unified App Architecture

  Single HTML Entry Point with Mode Switching

  Instead of separate HTML files, create a single index.html with a mode switcher:

  <!DOCTYPE html>
  <html>
  <head>
      <title>Bookchop - Document Processing Pipeline</title>
  </head>
  <body>
      <!-- Mode Navigation -->
      <nav class="mode-switcher">
          <button data-mode="annotate" class="active">📝 Mark Regions</button>
          <button data-mode="ocr">🔍 Extract Text</button>
          <button data-mode="view">📖 Present</button>
      </nav>

      <!-- Dynamic Content Area -->
      <div id="app-container">
          <!-- Mode-specific content loaded here -->
      </div>
  </body>
  </html>

  Modular TypeScript Architecture

  Keep the existing separation but coordinate them:

  src/
  ├── app.ts              # Main coordinator & mode switching
  ├── modes/
  │   ├── annotator.ts    # Mode 1 (current annotator.ts)
  │   ├── ocr.ts          # Mode 2 (new)
  │   └── viewer.ts       # Mode 3 (current viewer.ts)
  ├── shared/
  │   ├── pdf-utils.ts    # Shared PDF handling
  │   ├── data-manager.ts # .chaya file management
  │   └── ui-utils.ts     # Common UI components

  State Management Strategy

  Use a simple coordinator pattern:

  // src/app.ts
  class BookchopApp {
      private currentMode: 'annotate' | 'ocr' | 'view' = 'annotate';
      private documentState = {
          pdfFile: null,
          chayaData: null,
          currentStage: 1 // 1=regions, 2=+text, 3=presentation
      };

      async switchMode(newMode: string) {
          // Save current state
          await this.saveCurrentProgress();

          // Switch UI
          this.hideCurrentMode();
          this.showMode(newMode);

          // Load appropriate data for new mode
          await this.loadModeData(newMode);
      }

      private showMode(mode: string) {
          switch(mode) {
              case 'annotate':
                  import('./modes/annotator.js').then(m => m.initialize(this.documentState));
                  break;
              case 'ocr':
                  import('./modes/ocr.js').then(m => m.initialize(this.documentState));
                  break;
              case 'view':
                  import('./modes/viewer.js').then(m => m.initialize(this.documentState));
                  break;
          }
      }
  }

  Implementation Steps

  Phase 1: Minimal Unification

  1. Create index.html with mode tabs
  2. Move current annotator.ts content into modes/annotator.ts
  3. Move current viewer.ts content into modes/viewer.ts
  4. Create app.ts coordinator that shows/hides mode containers
  5. Keep existing functionality 100% intact

  Phase 2: Add Semantic Types to Mode 1

  Enhance the current annotator with:
  - Dropdown for semantic type selection during annotation creation
  - Icons in sidebar based on semantic type
  - Updated .chaya format with semanticType field

  Phase 3: Build Mode 2 (OCR)

  Create modes/ocr.ts with:
  - Region-by-region navigation
  - OCR text extraction and editing interface
  - Progress through all regions

  Phase 4: Enhance Mode 3

  Transform current viewer into presentation mode with:
  - Beautiful HTML rendering
  - Bidirectional linking
  - Export functionality

  Benefits of This Approach

  ✅ Keeps existing code intact - Your current annotator and viewer can be moved almost unchanged

  ✅ Clear separation - Each mode is still a separate module with its own concerns

  ✅ Shared infrastructure - PDF handling, file management, etc. can be shared

  ✅ Progressive enhancement - Can build this incrementally without breaking existing functionality

  ✅ Single deployable unit - One website instead of multiple pages

  ✅ State persistence - Document state carries through modes automatically

  File Organization

  bookchop/
  ├── index.html          # Unified entry point
  ├── annotator.html      # Keep for backward compatibility
  ├── viewer.html         # Keep for backward compatibility
  ├── src/
  │   ├── app.ts         # Main coordinator
  │   ├── modes/
  │   │   ├── annotator.ts
  │   │   ├── ocr.ts
  │   │   └── viewer.ts
  │   └── shared/
  │       ├── pdf-utils.ts
  │       ├── data-manager.ts
  │       └── ui-utils.ts

  This approach gives you the unified three-mode workflow while maintaining the clean separation between concerns. The existing annotator and viewer code can be preserved almost exactly as-is, just wrapped in a mode management system.

  TypeScript Architecture

  // src/app.ts - Main coordinator
  export class ChayaApp {
      private currentMode: 'annotate' | 'ocr' | 'view' = 'annotate';
      private documentState = new DocumentState();

      constructor() {
          this.initializeModeButtons();
          this.switchToMode('annotate');
      }

      private async switchToMode(mode: string) {
          // Hide all modes
          document.querySelectorAll('.mode-container').forEach(el =>
              el.classList.add('hidden'));

          // Show target mode
          document.getElementById(`${mode}-mode`)?.classList.remove('hidden');

          // Initialize mode-specific functionality
          switch(mode) {
              case 'annotate':
                  const { initializeAnnotator } = await import('./modes/annotator.js');
                  initializeAnnotator(this.documentState);
                  break;
              case 'ocr':
                  const { initializeOCR } = await import('./modes/ocr.js');
                  initializeOCR(this.documentState);
                  break;
              case 'view':
                  const { initializeViewer } = await import('./modes/viewer.js');
                  initializeViewer(this.documentState);
                  break;
          }
      }
  }

  // Initialize app
  new ChayaApp();

  ✅ Simpler deployment - One file, one URL
  ✅ Seamless mode switching - No page reloads
  ✅ Shared state - Document data persists across modes
  ✅ Better UX - Users see the full workflow progression
  ✅ Easier to maintain - Single entry point
  ✅ URL routing - Can add #annotate, #ocr, #view for direct links

  chaya.shreevatsa.net/#ocr       # Direct link to OCR mode

> Yes you've understood it correctly. Should everything be in a single JSON file, or should we have separate ones:

  annotations.json: regions of the PDF,

  ocr-edited.json: the overall markup, including regions and their OCR / headings, etc,
  OR
  ocr.json: the OCR for each individual region separately, 
  structure.json: the overall text: which region goes where etc,

⏺ Great question! This is a crucial architectural decision that will affect the entire workflow. Let me think through the tradeoffs:

  Option 1: Single JSON File

  {
    "metadata": {...},
    "regions": [
      {
        "id": "region1",
        "pageNumber": 1,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.2, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.1,
        "semanticType": "title",
        "label": "Chapter heading",
        // OCR data (added in Edit stage)
        "ocrText": "Chapter One",
        "editedText": "<h1>Chapter One</h1>",
        "ocrConfidence": 0.95
      }
    ],
    // Document structure (added in Edit stage)
    "documentStructure": {
      "title": "region1",
      "sections": [
        {
          "heading": "region2",
          "content": ["region3", "region4"]
        }
      ]
    }
  }

  Option 2: Separate Files

  annotations.json    # Just regions + spatial data
  ocr.json           # OCR text for each region
  structure.json     # Document hierarchy/ordering

  My Recommendation: Single JSON File

  Here's why:

  ✅ Simpler State Management

  - One source of truth
  - Atomic updates (no sync issues between files)
  - Easier to reason about data consistency

  ✅ Better for .chaya Format

  - Single file fits naturally in ZIP structure
  - Simpler manifest (just document.json instead of multiple files)
  - Easier versioning and migration

  ✅ Incremental Enhancement Pattern

  // Stage 1: Just regions
  region = { id, x, y, width, height, semanticType, label }

  // Stage 2: Add OCR data
  region = { ...region, ocrText, editedText, ocrConfidence }

  // Stage 3: Add document structure
  document = { regions: [...], documentStructure: {...} }

  ✅ Simpler API

  // Load everything at once
  const document = await loadDocument();

  // vs. coordinating multiple files
  const annotations = await loadAnnotations();
  const ocr = await loadOCR();
  const structure = await loadStructure();
  // Keep them in sync somehow...

  Proposed Schema Evolution

  Stage 1 (Mark): Basic regions

  {
    "version": "1.0",
    "metadata": {...},
    "regions": [
      {
        "id": "region1",
        "pageNumber": 1,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.2, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.1,
        "semanticType": "title",
        "label": "Chapter heading"
      }
    ]
  }

  Stage 2 (Edit): Add OCR + structure

  {
    "version": "2.0",
    "metadata": {...},
    "regions": [
      {
        "id": "region1",
        "pageNumber": 1,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.2, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.1,
        "semanticType": "title",
        "label": "Chapter heading",
        // New OCR fields
        "ocrText": "Chapter One",
        "editedText": "<h1>Chapter One</h1>",
        "ocrConfidence": 0.95,
        "lastEditedAt": "2025-07-16T10:30:00Z"
      }
    ],
    // New document structure
    "documentStructure": {
      "title": "region1",
      "sections": [...]
    }
  }

  Benefits

  - Backward compatible: v1.0 files still work, just missing OCR data
  - Forward compatible: Easy to add new fields as needed
  - Atomic operations: Save everything or nothing
  - Simple implementation: One file to load/save/sync

  The single file approach feels much more maintainable and aligns well with the progressive enhancement workflow you described. What do you think?

> Ok, single file is ok. But within the single file, I'd like to keep the regions and their text (or the overall text, making references to region ids) in separate fields of the JSON object.

  Proposed Structure

  {
    "version": "2.0",
    "metadata": {
      "sourcePdf": "document.pdf",
      "createdAt": "2025-07-16T10:00:00Z",
      "lastEditedAt": "2025-07-16T11:30:00Z"
    },

    // Spatial/layout data (from Mark stage)
    "regions": [
      {
        "id": "region1",
        "pageNumber": 1,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.2, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.1,
        "semanticType": "title",
        "label": "Chapter heading"
      },
      {
        "id": "region2",
        "pageNumber": 1,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.4, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.3,
        "semanticType": "paragraph",
        "label": "First paragraph"
      }
    ],

    // Textual/content data (from Edit stage)
    "content": {
      "regions": {
        "region1": {
          "ocrText": "Chapter One",
          "editedText": "<h1>Chapter One</h1>",
          "ocrConfidence": 0.95,
          "lastEditedAt": "2025-07-16T11:15:00Z"
        },
        "region2": {
          "ocrText": "In the beginning was the word...",
          "editedText": "<p>In the beginning was the word...</p>",
          "ocrConfidence": 0.88,
          "lastEditedAt": "2025-07-16T11:20:00Z"
        }
      },

      // Document structure/ordering
      "structure": {
        "type": "document",
        "title": "region1",
        "sections": [
          {
            "type": "chapter",
            "heading": "region1",
            "content": ["region2", "region3", "region4"]
          }
        ]
      }
    }
  }

  Benefits of This Structure

  ✅ Clean Separation

  - regions[]: Pure spatial/layout data (Mark stage)
  - content.regions{}: Pure textual data (Edit stage)
  - content.structure{}: Document organization (Edit stage)

  ✅ Easy Stage-by-Stage Development

  // Stage 1: Only regions exist
  const doc = { regions: [...] };

  // Stage 2: Add content fields
  const doc = { regions: [...], content: { regions: {...}, structure: {...} } };

  ✅ Flexible References

  - Content can reference regions by ID
  - Structure can reorder/reorganize without touching spatial data
  - Easy to find "orphaned" regions (in regions[] but not in content.regions{})

  ✅ Independent Evolution

  - Can update OCR without touching layout
  - Can restructure document without re-OCRing
  - Can add new regions without affecting existing content

  Data Flow Examples

  Mark Stage → Edit Stage

  // User marks regions
  regions = [{id: "region1", x: 0.1, ...}];

  // OCR processes each region
  content.regions["region1"] = {
    ocrText: await ocrRegion("region1"),
    editedText: ocrText, // initially same
    ocrConfidence: 0.95
  };

  View Stage Toggle

  function getRegionDisplay(regionId, mode) {
    const region = regions.find(r => r.id === regionId);
    const content = content.regions[regionId];

    return mode === 'original'
      ? extractPdfCrop(region)  // Show PDF crop
      : content?.editedText;    // Show enhanced text
  }

  This structure feels really clean and extensible. Does this capture what you had in mind?

> More or less, yes. Do you know how Wikisource does it? How does it represent the text of a work, with reference to the pages it contains etc?

⏺ Great question! Wikisource has a really sophisticated approach that's quite relevant to what you're building.

  Wikisource's Approach

  Wikisource uses a two-layer system:

  1. Page Namespace (Layout/Source)

  Each page of the source document gets its own wiki page:
  - Page:Book_Title.pdf/1 - First page
  - Page:Book_Title.pdf/2 - Second page
  - etc.

  Each page contains:
  <noinclude>
  {{PageQuality|1|user}}
  {{RunningHeader|left=Chapter 1|center=|right=23}}
  </noinclude>

  <section begin=chapter1 />
  = Chapter One =

  In the beginning was the word, and the word was with God...
  <section end=chapter1 />

  <section begin=paragraph1 />
  This is the first paragraph of the chapter content...
  <section end=paragraph1 />

  2. Main Namespace (Logical/Reading)

  The actual readable work is assembled by transcluding sections from pages:
  {{header|title=Book Title|author=Author Name}}

  == Chapter 1 ==
  {{Page:Book_Title.pdf/23|chapter1}}

  {{Page:Book_Title.pdf/23|paragraph1}}
  {{Page:Book_Title.pdf/24|paragraph1_continued}}

  Key Insights from Wikisource

  ✅ Separation of Concerns

  - Pages: Focus on faithful transcription of source layout
  - Sections: Named regions that can span page boundaries
  - Works: Logical reading order, can reorder/reorganize sections

  ✅ Named Sections with Boundaries

  - <section begin=name /> and <section end=name />
  - Allows precise references to text spans
  - Text can span multiple pages
  - Same text can be included in multiple contexts

  ✅ Flexible Assembly

  - Final document built by referencing sections
  - Can create different "views" (complete work, chapter only, etc.)
  - Can handle complex layouts (footnotes, multi-column, etc.)

  Adapting This to Your Approach

  This maps really well to your structure! You could extend it like:

  {
    "regions": [
      {
        "id": "region1",
        "pageNumber": 23,
        "x": 0.1, "y": 0.2, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.1,
        "semanticType": "title",
        "label": "Chapter heading",
        "sectionName": "chapter1_title"  // Named reference
      }
    ],

    "content": {
      "sections": {
        "chapter1_title": {
          "regions": ["region1"],
          "ocrText": "Chapter One",
          "editedText": "<h1>Chapter One</h1>"
        },
        "chapter1_intro": {
          "regions": ["region2", "region3"],  // Spans multiple regions!
          "ocrText": "In the beginning...",
          "editedText": "<p>In the beginning was the word...</p>"
        }
      },

      "structure": {
        "title": "Book Title",
        "chapters": [
          {
            "title": "chapter1_title",
            "content": ["chapter1_intro", "chapter1_body"]
          }
        ]
      }
    }
  }

  Benefits of Section-Based Approach

  - Logical units: Sections represent meaningful text units
  - Flexible spanning: One section can include multiple regions
  - Reusable content: Same section can appear in multiple contexts
  - Clean references: Structure references sections, not individual regions

  This feels like it could give you the same flexibility as Wikisource while maintaining your clean separation between spatial and textual data. What do you think of this section-based approach?

> Yes something like this is what I had in mind. Or maybe a Prosemirror document that contains
